**Canada**[^cite_note-9] is a country in [North America](21139.md). Its [ten provinces and three territories](75763.md) extend from the Atlantic Ocean to the Pacific Ocean and northward into the Arctic Ocean, making it the [second-largest country by total area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_and_dependencies_by_area), with the [longest coastline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_length_of_coastline) of any country. Its [border with the United States](630260.md) is the longest international land border. The country is characterized by a wide range of both [meteorologic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temperature_in_Canada) and [geological](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Canada) regions. With [a population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Population_of_Canada) of over 41 million, it has widely varying population densities, with the majority residing in [its urban areas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_the_largest_population_centres_in_Canada) and large areas being sparsely populated. Its capital is [Ottawa](22219.md) and [its three largest metropolitan areas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_census_metropolitan_areas_and_agglomerations_in_Canada) are [Toronto](64646.md), [Montreal](7954681.md), and [Vancouver](32706.md).

[Indigenous peoples](196075.md) have continuously inhabited what is now Canada for thousands of years. Beginning in the 16th century, [British](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_colonization_of_the_Americas) and [French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_colonization_of_the_Americas) expeditions explored and later settled along the Atlantic coast. As a consequence of [various armed conflicts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_history_of_Canada), France [ceded nearly all](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Paris_\(1763\)) of [its colonies in North America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_France) in 1763. In 1867, with the union of three [British North American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_North_America) colonies through [Confederation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_Confederation), Canada was formed as a [federal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federalism) [dominion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominion) of four provinces. This began an [accretion of provinces and territories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territorial_evolution_of_Canada) resulting in the [displacement of Indigenous populations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Numbered_Treaties), and a process of increasing autonomy from the United Kingdom. This increased sovereignty was highlighted by the *[Statute of Westminster, 1931](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statute_of_Westminster_1931)*, and culminated in the *[Canada Act 1982](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canada_Act_1982)*, which severed the vestiges of legal dependence on the [Parliament of the United Kingdom](13964.md).

Canada is a [parliamentary democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_system) and a [constitutional monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_monarchy) in the [Westminster tradition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Westminster_system). The country's [head of government](37570.md) is the [prime minister](24135.md), who holds office by virtue of their ability to [command the confidence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confidence_and_supply) of the elected [House of Commons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Commons_of_Canada) and is appointed by the [governor general](38152.md), representing the [monarch of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monarchy_of_Canada), the ceremonial [head of state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Head_of_state). The country is a [Commonwealth realm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_realm) and is [officially bilingual](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Official_bilingualism_in_Canada) (English and French) in the federal jurisdiction. It is [very highly ranked in international measurements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_rankings_of_Canada) of government transparency, quality of life, economic competitiveness, innovation, education and human rights. It is one of the world's most [ethnically diverse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_origins_of_people_in_Canada) and [multicultural](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multiculturalism_in_Canada) nations, the product of [large-scale immigration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_to_Canada). Canada's long and complex [relationship with the United States](5199.md) has had a significant impact on [its history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Canada), [economy](5195.md), and [culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Canada).

A [developed country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Developed_country), Canada's advanced economy has a [high nominal per capita GDP globally](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(nominal\)_per_capita) and ranks among the [largest in the world by nominal GDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(nominal\)), relying chiefly upon [its abundant natural resources](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Canada#Natural_resources) and well-developed [international trade networks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_the_largest_trading_partners_of_Canada). Canada is recognized as a [middle power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_power); its support for [multilateralism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multilateralism) and [internationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internationalism_\(politics\)) has been closely related to [its foreign policies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_Canada) of [peacekeeping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_peacekeeping) and [aid for developing countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_foreign_aid). Canada promotes [its domestically shared values](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_values) through participation in [multiple international organizations and forums](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_organisation_membership_of_Canada).

## Etymology

Main article: [Name of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Name_of_Canada)

While a variety of theories have been postulated for the etymological origins of *Canada*, the name is now accepted as coming from the [St. Lawrence Iroquoian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Lawrence_Iroquoian) word *kanata*, meaning 'village' or 'settlement'.[^cite_note-10] In 1535, Indigenous inhabitants of the present-day [Quebec City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quebec_City) region used the word to direct French explorer [Jacques Cartier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacques_Cartier) to the village of [Stadacona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stadacona).[^cite_note-Rayburn2001-11] Cartier later used the word *Canada* to refer not only to that particular village but to the entire area subject to [Donnacona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donnacona) (the chief at Stadacona);[^cite_note-Rayburn2001-11] by 1545, European books and maps had begun referring to this small region along the [Saint Lawrence River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Lawrence_River) as *Canada*.[^cite_note-Rayburn2001-11]

From the 16th to the early 18th century, *[Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canada_\(New_France\))* referred to the part of [New France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_France) that lay along the Saint Lawrence River.[^cite_note-12] Following the British [conquest of New France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conquest_of_New_France), this area was known as the British [Province of Quebec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Province_of_Quebec_\(1763%E2%80%931791\)) from 1763 to 1791.[^cite_note-f014-13] In 1791, the area became two British colonies called [Upper Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_Canada) and [Lower Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lower_Canada). These two colonies were collectively referred to as [the Canadas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Canadas) until their union as the [Province of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Province_of_Canada) in 1841.[^cite_note-14]

Upon [Confederation in 1867](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_Confederation), *Canada* was adopted as the legal name for the new country at the [London Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/London_Conference_of_1866) and the word *[dominion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominion)* was conferred as the country's title, reflecting its status as a [self-governing nation within the British Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire#Changing_status_of_the_white_colonies).[^cite_note-m594-15] Following the [London Declaration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/London_Declaration) of 1949, where it was agreed that India could be a full member of the [Commonwealth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Nations) as a republic, not a dominion, the leaders of the Commonwealth countries agreed that the other members of the Commonwealth should not continue to be referred to as *dominions*. In 1951, Prime Minister [Louis St. Laurent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_St._Laurent) announced in the [Canadian House of Commons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_House_of_Commons) that in light of the *[Statute of Westminster, 1931](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statute_of_Westminster_1931)*, and the changes to the Commonwealth, *dominion* was no longer an accurate term to use to refer to Canada and would no longer be used by the federal government.[^cite_note-16] Similarly by 1952, the United Kingdom no longer used the term *dominions* to refer to Canada or other members of the Commonwealth,[^cite_note-u285-17] instead beginning to use the term a *[realm of the Commonwealth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_realm)*.[^cite_note-18]

The *[Canada Act 1982](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canada_Act_1982)*, which brought the [Constitution of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Canada) fully under Canadian control, referred only to *Canada*. Later that year, the name of the national holiday was changed from Dominion Day to [Canada Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canada_Day).[^cite_note-buckner-19]

## History

Main article: [History of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Canada)

Further information: [Timeline of Canadian history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_Canadian_history) and [Historiography of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historiography_of_Canada)

### Indigenous peoples

&#x20;The [first inhabitants of North America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Settlement_of_the_Americas) are generally hypothesized to have migrated from [Siberia](29639.md) by way of the [Bering land bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beringia) and arrived at least 14,000 years ago.[^cite_note-20] The [Paleo-Indian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paleo-Indian) archaeological sites at [Old Crow Flats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Crow_Flats) and [Bluefish Caves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bluefish_Caves) are two of the oldest sites of human habitation in Canada.[^cite_note-21] The [characteristics of Indigenous societies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Technological_and_industrial_history_of_Canada#The_Stone_Age:_Fire_\(14,000_BC_%E2%80%93_AD_1600\)) included permanent settlements, agriculture, complex societal hierarchies, and trading networks.[^cite_note-22] Some of these cultures had collapsed by the time European explorers arrived in the late 15th and early 16th centuries and have only been discovered through archaeological investigations.[^cite_note-23] [Indigenous peoples in present-day Canada](196075.md) include the [First Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Nations_in_Canada), [Inuit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inuit), and [Métis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C3%A9tis_people_in_Canada),[^cite_note-GraberKuprecht2012-24] the last being of [mixed descent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mixed-blood) who originated in the mid-17th century when First Nations people married European settlers and their offspring subsequently developed their own identity.[^cite_note-GraberKuprecht2012-24]



A map of Canada showing the percent of [self-reported indigenous identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Population_of_Canada#Indigenous_peoples) (First Nations, Inuit, Métis) by census division, according to the [2021 Canadian census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Canadian_census)

[^cite_note-Statistics_Canada_2022_u867-25]

The [Indigenous population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Population_history_of_indigenous_peoples_of_the_Americas) at the time of the first European settlements is estimated to have been between 200,000[^cite_note-dying-26] and two million,[^cite_note-27] with a figure of 500,000 accepted by Canada's [Royal Commission on Aboriginal Peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Commission_on_Aboriginal_Peoples).[^cite_note-28] As a consequence of European colonization, the Indigenous population declined by forty to eighty percent.[^cite_note-29] The decline is attributed to several causes, including the [transfer of European diseases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Columbian_exchange), to which they had no natural immunity,[^cite_note-30] conflicts over the fur trade, conflicts with the colonial authorities and settlers, and the loss of Indigenous lands to settlers and the subsequent collapse of several nations' self-sufficiency.[^cite_note-31]

Although not without conflict, [European Canadians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Canadians)' early interactions with First Nations and Inuit populations were relatively peaceful.[^cite_note-32] First Nations and Métis peoples played a critical part in the development of [European colonies in Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Former_colonies_and_territories_in_Canada), particularly for their role in assisting European [coureurs des bois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coureur_des_bois) and [voyageurs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voyageurs) in their explorations of the continent during the [North American fur trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_American_fur_trade).[^cite_note-33] These early European interactions with First Nations would change from [friendship and peace treaties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peace_and_Friendship_Treaties) to the [dispossession of Indigenous lands through treaties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Numbered_Treaties).[^cite_note-34]

[Settler colonialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Settler_colonialism_in_Canada) reached a peak in the late 19th and early 20th centuries.[^cite_note-35] The [Canadian government](616255.md) funded [Indian residential schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_Indian_residential_school_system), administered by Christian churches, that are considered to be part of a [genocide](12441.md) against Indigenous people due to the institutions' use of [forced assimilation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forced_assimilation).[^cite_note-u159-36] In 2021, [possible gravesites of Indigenous children](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_Indian_residential_school_gravesites) were found near former Canadian residential schools, highlighting the [cultural genocide against Indigenous peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_genocide_of_Indigenous_peoples).[^cite_note-w624-37] A period of redress began with the formation of a [reconciliation commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Truth_and_Reconciliation_Commission_of_Canada) by the Government of Canada in 2008.[^cite_note-trc-38] This included acknowledgment of a cultural genocide,[^cite_note-TRCSummary-39] [settlement agreements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Residential_Schools_Settlement_Agreement),[^cite_note-trc-38] and betterment of racial discrimination issues, such as addressing the plight of [missing and murdered Indigenous women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Missing_and_murdered_Indigenous_women).[^cite_note-Ministère_de_la_Justice_2017-40]

### European colonization



Map of territorial claims in [North America](21139.md) by 1750. Possessions of [British America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_America) (pink), [New France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_France) (blue), and [New Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Spain) (orange); California, Pacific Northwest, and Great Basin not indicated.

[^cite_note-Chapman_p.-41]

It is believed that the first documented European to explore the east coast of Canada was [Norse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vikings) explorer [Leif Erikson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leif_Erikson).[^cite_note-42] In approximately 1000 AD, the Norse built a small short-lived encampment that was occupied sporadically for perhaps 20 years at [L'Anse aux Meadows](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L'Anse_aux_Meadows) on the northern tip of [Newfoundland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newfoundland).[^cite_note-43] No further European exploration occurred until 1497, when seafarer [John Cabot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Cabot) explored and claimed Canada's [Atlantic coast](71095.md) in the name of [Henry VII of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_VII_of_England).[^cite_note-BlakeKeshen2017p19-44] In 1534, French explorer [Jacques Cartier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacques_Cartier) explored the [Gulf of Saint Lawrence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_of_Saint_Lawrence) where, on July 24, he planted a 10-metre (33 ft) cross bearing the words, "long live the King of France", and took possession of the territory New France in the name of [King Francis I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francis_I_of_France).[^cite_note-45] The early 16th century saw European mariners with navigational techniques pioneered by the [Basque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basques) and [Portuguese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_discoveries) establish seasonal whaling and fishing outposts along the Atlantic coast.[^cite_note-46] In general, early settlements appear to have been [short-lived](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Population_of_Canada#Ephemeral_European_settlements) due to a combination of the harsh climate, problems with navigating trade routes and competing outputs in Scandinavia. [^cite_note-47]

In 1583, Sir [Humphrey Gilbert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humphrey_Gilbert), by the [royal prerogative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_prerogative) of Queen [Elizabeth I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elizabeth_I), founded [St John's, Newfoundland](70581.md), as the first North American [English seasonal camp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_overseas_possessions).[^cite_note-48] In 1600, the French established their first seasonal trading post at [Tadoussac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tadoussac) along the Saint Lawrence.[^cite_note-CordellLightfoot2008-49] French explorer [Samuel de Champlain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_de_Champlain) arrived in 1603 and established the first permanent year-round European settlements at [Port Royal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port-Royal_\(Acadia\)) (in 1605) and Quebec City (in 1608).[^cite_note-50] Among the [colonists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_colonization_of_the_Americas) of New France, *[Canadiens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Canadians)* extensively settled the Saint Lawrence River valley and [Acadians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acadians) settled the present-day [Maritimes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Maritimes), while fur traders and [Catholic missionaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_and_the_Age_of_Discovery) explored the [Great Lakes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Lakes), [Hudson Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hudson_Bay), and the [Mississippi watershed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mississippi_watershed) to [Louisiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_\(New_France\)).[^cite_note-51] The [Beaver Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beaver_Wars) broke out in the mid-17th century over control of the North American fur trade.[^cite_note-52]

The English established additional settlements in [Newfoundland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newfoundland_\(island\)) in 1610 along with settlements in the [Thirteen Colonies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thirteen_Colonies) to the south.[^cite_note-53] A series of [four wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_and_Indian_Wars) erupted in colonial North America between 1689 and 1763; the later wars of the period constituted the North American theatre of the [Seven Years' War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seven_Years'_War).[^cite_note-54] Mainland [Nova Scotia](21184.md) came under British rule with the 1713 [Treaty of Utrecht](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Utrecht) and Canada and most of New France came under British rule in 1763 after the Seven Years' War.[^cite_note-55]

### British North America

Painting of General Wolfe dying in front of the British flag while attended by officers and native allies

[Benjamin West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benjamin_West)'s *[The Death of General Wolfe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Death_of_General_Wolfe)* (1771) dramatizes [James Wolfe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Wolfe)'s death during the [Battle of the Plains of Abraham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_the_Plains_of_Abraham) at [Quebec City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quebec_City).

[^cite_note-National_Gallery_of_Canada_n963-56]

The [Royal Proclamation of 1763](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Proclamation_of_1763) established First Nation treaty rights, created the [Province of Quebec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Province_of_Quebec_\(1763%E2%80%931791\)) out of New France, and annexed [Cape Breton Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_Breton_Island) to Nova Scotia.[^cite_note-buckner-19] St John's Island (now [Prince Edward Island](23071.md)) became a separate colony in 1769.[^cite_note-57] To avert conflict in [Quebec](7954867.md), the British Parliament passed the [Quebec Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quebec_Act) 1774, expanding Quebec's territory to the Great Lakes and [Ohio Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ohio_River).[^cite_note-58] More importantly, the Quebec Act afforded Quebec special autonomy and rights of self-administration at a time when the Thirteen Colonies were increasingly agitating against British rule.[^cite_note-59] It re-established the French language, Catholic faith, and [French civil law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_of_France) there, staving off the growth of an independence movement in contrast to the Thirteen Colonies.[^cite_note-60] The Proclamation and the Quebec Act in turn angered many residents of the Thirteen Colonies, further fuelling anti-British sentiment in the years prior to the [American Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Revolution).[^cite_note-buckner-19]

After the American War of Independence, the [1783 Treaty of Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Paris_\(1783\)) recognized the independence of the [United States](3434750.md) and set the terms of peace, ceding [British North American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_North_America) territories south of the Great Lakes and east of the Mississippi River to the new country.[^cite_note-61] The American war of independence also caused a large out-migration of [Loyalists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loyalists), the settlers who had fought against American independence. Many moved to Canada, particularly Atlantic Canada, where their arrival changed the demographic distribution of the existing territories. [New Brunswick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Brunswick) was in turn split from Nova Scotia as part of a reorganization of Loyalist settlements in the Maritimes, which led to the incorporation of [Saint John, New Brunswick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_John,_New_Brunswick), as Canada's first city.[^cite_note-62] To accommodate the influx of English-speaking Loyalists in Central Canada, the [Constitutional Act of 1791](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_Act_1791) divided the Province of Quebec into French-speaking Lower Canada (later [Quebec](7954867.md)) and English-speaking Upper Canada (later [Ontario](22218.md#Canada_West_\(1841%E2%80%931867\))), granting each its own elected legislative assembly.[^cite_note-63]

Painting of Laura Secord warning British commander James FitzGibbon of an impending American attack at Beaver Dams

[War of 1812](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_of_1812) heroine [Laura Secord](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laura_Secord) warning British commander [James FitzGibbon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_FitzGibbon) of an [impending American attack at Beaver Dams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Beaver_Dams)

[^cite_note-Collection_Search_2023_k800-64]

The Canadas were the main front in the [War of 1812](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_of_1812) between the United States and the [United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Kingdom_of_Great_Britain_and_Ireland). Peace came in 1815; no boundaries were changed.[^cite_note-65] Immigration resumed at a higher level, with over 960,000 arrivals from Britain between 1815 and 1850.[^cite_note-66] New arrivals included refugees escaping the [Great Irish Famine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Irish_Famine) as well as [Gaelic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Gaelic)-speaking Scots displaced by the [Highland Clearances](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Highland_Clearances).[^cite_note-67] Infectious diseases killed between 25 and 33 percent of Europeans who immigrated to Canada before 1891.[^cite_note-dying-26]

The desire for [responsible government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Responsible_government) resulted in the abortive [Rebellions of 1837](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rebellions_of_1837).[^cite_note-68] The [Durham Report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Durham_Report) subsequently recommended responsible government and the assimilation of French Canadians into English culture.[^cite_note-buckner-19] The [Act of Union 1840](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Act_of_Union_1840) merged the Canadas into a united Province of Canada and responsible government was established for all provinces of British North America east of Lake Superior by 1855.[^cite_note-69] The signing of the [Oregon Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oregon_Treaty) by Britain and the United States in 1846 ended the [Oregon boundary dispute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oregon_boundary_dispute), extending the border westward along the [49th parallel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/49th_parallel_north). This paved the way for British colonies on [Vancouver Island (1849)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colony_of_Vancouver_Island) and in [British Columbia (1858)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colony_of_British_Columbia_\(1858%E2%80%931866\)).[^cite_note-70] The Anglo-Russian [Treaty of Saint Petersburg (1825)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Saint_Petersburg_\(1825\)) established the border along the Pacific coast, but, even after the US [Alaska Purchase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaska_Purchase) of 1867, disputes continued about the exact demarcation of the Alaska–Yukon and Alaska–British Columbia border.[^cite_note-71]

### Confederation and expansion

Refer to caption

Animated map showing [the growth and change of Canada's provinces and territories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territorial_evolution_of_Canada) since Confederation in 1867

[^cite_note-Natural_Resources_Canada_2016_m242-72]

Following three constitutional conferences, the *[British North America Act, 1867](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_Act,_1867)* officially proclaimed Canadian Confederation on July 1, 1867, initially with four provinces: [Ontario](22218.md), Quebec, Nova Scotia, and New Brunswick.[^cite_note-73] Canada assumed control of [Rupert's Land](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rupert's_Land) and the [North-Western Territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North-Western_Territory) to form the [Northwest Territories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northwest_Territories), where the Métis' grievances ignited the [Red River Rebellion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_River_Rebellion) and the creation of the province of [Manitoba](18926.md) in July 1870.[^cite_note-74] British Columbia and Vancouver Island (which [had been united](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colony_of_British_Columbia_\(1866%E2%80%931871\)) in 1866) joined the confederation in 1871 on the promise of a transcontinental railway extending to Victoria in the province within 10 years,[^cite_note-75] while Prince Edward Island joined in 1873.[^cite_note-canatlas-76] In 1898, during the [Klondike Gold Rush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Klondike_Gold_Rush) in the Northwest Territories, Parliament created the Yukon Territory. [Alberta](717.md) and [Saskatchewan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saskatchewan) became provinces in 1905.[^cite_note-canatlas-76] Between 1871 and 1896, almost one quarter of the Canadian population emigrated south to the US.[^cite_note-77]

To settle [the West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Canada) and attract European immigrants, Canada sponsored the construction of three transcontinental railways, enacted the *[Dominion Lands Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominion_Lands_Act)* and founded the [North-West Mounted Police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North-West_Mounted_Police) to maintain regional authority.[^cite_note-78] This [period of westward expansion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post-Confederation_Canada_\(1867%E2%80%931914\)#Expansion) and [nation building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Policy) resulted in the displacement of many [Indigenous peoples of the Canadian Prairies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plains_Indians) to "[Indian reserves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_reserve)",[^cite_note-79] clearing the way for ethnic European [block settlements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Block_settlement).[^cite_note-80] This caused the collapse of the [Plains Bison in western Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_bison_conservation_in_Canada#Plains_bison) and the introduction of European [cattle farms and wheat fields](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agriculture_in_Canada) dominating the land.[^cite_note-81] The Indigenous peoples saw widespread famine and disease due to the loss of the bison and their traditional hunting lands.[^cite_note-82] The federal government did provide emergency relief, on condition of the Indigenous peoples moving to the reserves.[^cite_note-83] During this time, Canada introduced the *[Indian Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Act)* unilaterally extending its control over the First Nations to education, government and legal rights.[^cite_note-84]

### Early 20th century

1918 [Canadian War bond posters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canada_Savings_Bond) depicting three Frenchwomen pulling a [harrow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drag_harrow)



English: They serve France. How can I serve Canada? Buy Victory Bonds.



French: They serve France. Everyone can be of service. Buy Victory Bonds.

Because Britain still maintained control of Canada's foreign affairs under the British North America Act, 1867, its declaration of war in 1914 automatically brought [Canada into the First World War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_history_of_Canada_during_World_War_I).[^cite_note-85] Volunteers sent to the [Western Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Front_\(World_War_I\)) later became part of the [Canadian Corps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_Corps), which played a substantial role in the [Battle of Vimy Ridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Vimy_Ridge) and other major engagements of the war.[^cite_note-morton-milhist-86] The [Conscription Crisis of 1917](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conscription_Crisis_of_1917) erupted when the [Unionist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unionist_Party_\(Canada\)) Cabinet's proposal to augment the military's dwindling number of active members with [conscription](5735.md) was met with vehement objections from French-speaking Quebecers.[^cite_note-McGonigal1962-87] In 1919, Canada joined the [League of Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/League_of_Nations) independently of Britain.[^cite_note-morton-milhist-86] After the 1926 [King–Byng affair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King%E2%80%93Byng_affair) raised concerns over the governor general's use of [reserve powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reserve_power), the [Balfour Declaration that year](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balfour_Declaration_of_1926) put Canada on a legal path to full sovereignty equal to Britain's, and the *[Statute of Westminster, 1931](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statute_of_Westminster,_1931)*, which implemented the declaration, affirmed Canada's independence.[^cite_note-88]

The [Great Depression in Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Depression_in_Canada) during the early 1930s saw an economic downturn, leading to hardship across the country.[^cite_note-89] In response to the downturn, the [Co-operative Commonwealth Federation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Co-operative_Commonwealth_Federation) (CCF) in Saskatchewan introduced many elements of a [welfare state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welfare_state) (as pioneered by [Tommy Douglas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tommy_Douglas)) in the 1940s and 1950s.[^cite_note-90] On the advice of Prime Minister [William Lyon Mackenzie King](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King), [war with Germany was declared](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Declaration_of_war_by_Canada#Nazi_Germany) effective September 10, 1939, by King [George VI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_VI), seven days after the United Kingdom. The delay underscored Canada's independence.[^cite_note-morton-milhist-86]

The first Canadian Army units arrived in Britain in December 1939. In all, over a million Canadians served in the armed forces during the [Second World War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_World_War).[^cite_note-91] Canadian troops played important roles in many key battles of the war, including the failed 1942 [Dieppe Raid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dieppe_Raid), the [Allied invasion of Italy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allied_invasion_of_Italy), the [Normandy landings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Normandy_landings), the [Battle of Normandy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Overlord), and the [Battle of the Scheldt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_the_Scheldt) in 1944.[^cite_note-morton-milhist-86] Canada provided asylum for the [Dutch monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_monarchy) while that country was [occupied](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reichskommissariat_Niederlande) and is credited by the Netherlands for major contributions to [its liberation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberation_Day_\(Netherlands\)) from [Nazi Germany](21212.md).[^cite_note-netherlands-92]

### Contemporary era

Despite another [conscription crisis in Quebec in 1944](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conscription_Crisis_of_1944), Canada finished the war with a large army and strong economy.[^cite_note-93] After two [referendums](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1948_Newfoundland_referendums), Newfoundlanders voted to join Canada in 1949 as a province.[^cite_note-94] Canada's post-war economic growth, combined with the policies of successive Liberal governments, led to the emergence of a new [Canadian identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_identity), marked by the adoption of the [maple leaf flag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flag_of_Canada) in 1965,[^cite_note-95] the implementation of [official bilingualism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Official_bilingualism) (English and French) in 1969,[^cite_note-96] and the institution of [official multiculturalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multiculturalism#Canada) in 1971.[^cite_note-97] [Socially democratic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socially_democratic) programs were also instituted, such as [Medicare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicare_\(Canada\)), the [Canada Pension Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canada_Pension_Plan), and [Canada Student Loans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Student_financial_aid_in_Canada), though provincial governments, particularly Quebec and Alberta, opposed many of these as incursions into their jurisdictions.[^cite_note-98]

refer to caption

A copy of the *[Canadian Charter of Rights and Freedoms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_Charter_of_Rights_and_Freedoms)*

[^cite_note-Ministère_de_la_Justice_2021_d986-99]

Finally, another series of constitutional conferences resulted in the *Canada Act 1982*, the [patriation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriation) of Canada's constitution from the United Kingdom, concurrent with the creation of the *[Canadian Charter of Rights and Freedoms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_Charter_of_Rights_and_Freedoms)*.[^cite_note-100] Canada had established complete sovereignty as an independent country under [its own monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monarchy_of_Canada).[^cite_note-101] In 1999, [Nunavut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nunavut) became Canada's third territory after a series of negotiations with the federal government.[^cite_note-102]

At the same time, Quebec underwent profound social and economic changes through the [Quiet Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quiet_Revolution) of the 1960s, giving birth to a secular [nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quebec_nationalism) movement.[^cite_note-103] The radical [Front de libération du Québec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Front_de_lib%C3%A9ration_du_Qu%C3%A9bec) (FLQ) ignited the [October Crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/October_Crisis) with a series of bombings and kidnappings in 1970,[^cite_note-104] and the [sovereigntist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quebec_sovereignty_movement) [Parti Québécois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parti_Qu%C3%A9b%C3%A9cois) was elected in 1976, organizing an [unsuccessful referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1980_Quebec_referendum) on sovereignty-association in 1980. Attempts to accommodate Quebec nationalism constitutionally through the [Meech Lake Accord](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meech_Lake_Accord) failed in 1990.[^cite_note-sorens-105] This led to the formation of the [Bloc Québécois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bloc_Qu%C3%A9b%C3%A9cois) in Quebec and the invigoration of the [Reform Party of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reform_Party_of_Canada) in the West.[^cite_note-106] A [second referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1995_Quebec_referendum) followed in 1995, in which sovereignty was rejected by a slimmer margin of 50.6 to 49.4 percent.[^cite_note-107] In 1997, the [Supreme Court](123468.md) ruled [unilateral secession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reference_Re_Secession_of_Quebec) by a province would be unconstitutional, and the [Clarity Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clarity_Act) was passed by Parliament, outlining the terms of a negotiated departure from Confederation.[^cite_note-sorens-105]

In addition to the issues of Quebec sovereignty, a number of crises shook Canadian society in the late 1980s and early 1990s. These included the explosion of [Air India Flight 182](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_India_Flight_182) in 1985, the largest mass murder in Canadian history;[^cite_note-108] the [École Polytechnique massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89cole_Polytechnique_massacre) in 1989, a [university shooting](59628.md) targeting female students;[^cite_note-109] and the [Oka Crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oka_Crisis) of 1990,[^cite_note-110] the first of a number of violent confrontations between provincial governments and Indigenous groups.[^cite_note-111] Canada joined the [Gulf War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_War) in 1990 and was active in [several peacekeeping missions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Canadian_peacekeeping_missions) in the 1990s, including operations in the [Balkans](4829.md) during and after the [Yugoslav Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yugoslav_Wars),[^cite_note-Defence_2016_d710-112] and in [Somalia](27358.md), resulting in an incident that has been described as "[the darkest era in the history of the Canadian military](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Somalia_affair)".[^cite_note-113]

After the [September 11 attacks](5058690.md) in the United States, Canada sent [troops to Afghanistan in 2001](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canada_in_the_War_in_Afghanistan), resulting in the [largest amount of Canadian deaths](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_Forces_casualties_in_Afghanistan) for any single military mission since the [Korean War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canada_in_the_Korean_War) in the early 1950s.[^cite_note-114] In 2011, Canadian forces participated in the [NATO](21133.md)-led intervention into the [Libyan Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Libyan_Civil_War)[^cite_note-115] and also became involved in battling the [Islamic State](9087364.md) insurgency in Iraq in the mid-2010s.[^cite_note-116] The [COVID-19 pandemic in Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Canada) began on January 27, 2020, causing widespread social and economic disruption.[^cite_note-117] A [trade war involving the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_United_States_trade_war_with_Canada) began on February 1, 2025, when U.S. president [Donald Trump](4848272.md) signed [orders imposing tariffs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Trump_tariffs) on goods entering the United States, alongside rhetoric suggesting the [annexation of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Movements_for_the_annexation_of_Canada_to_the_United_States#Proposals_to_annex_Canada_by_Donald_Trump).[^cite_note-y519-118]

## Geography

Main article: [Geography of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Canada)

refer to caption

A topographic map of Canada, in polar projection (for 90° W), showing elevations shaded from green to brown (higher)

By total area (including its waters), Canada is the [second-largest country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_and_dependencies_by_area).[^cite_note-119] By land area alone, Canada ranks fourth, due to having the world's largest area of [fresh water lakes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_lakes_of_Canada).[^cite_note-120] Stretching from the Atlantic Ocean in the east, along the Arctic Ocean to the north, and to the Pacific Ocean in the west, the country encompasses 9,984,670 square kilometres (3,855,100 sq mi) of territory.[^cite_note-McColl2005-121] Canada also has vast maritime terrain, with the world's longest coastline of 243,042 kilometres (151,019 mi)[^cite_note-International_Boundary_Commission-122] In addition to sharing [the world's largest land border with the United States](630260.md)—spanning 8,891 kilometres (5,525 mi)[^cite_note-canadausa-123]—Canada shares a short (1,280 metres (4,200 ft)) land border with [Greenland](12118.md) (and hence the [Kingdom of Denmark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Denmark)) to the northeast, on [Hans Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hans_Island),[^cite_note-124] and a [maritime boundary](29613061.md) with [France](5843419.md)'s [overseas collectivity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overseas_collectivity) of [Saint Pierre and Miquelon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Pierre_and_Miquelon) to the southeast.[^cite_note-125] Canada is also home to the world's northernmost settlement, [Canadian Forces Station Alert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CFS_Alert), on the northern tip of [Ellesmere Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ellesmere_Island)—latitude 82.5°N—which lies 817 kilometres (508 mi) from the North Pole.[^cite_note-126] In latitude, Canada's most northerly point of land is [Cape Columbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_Columbia) in Nunavut at 83°6′41″N, with its southern extreme at [Middle Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Island_\(Lake_Erie\)) in Lake Erie at 41°40′53″N. In longitude, Canada's land extends from [Cape Spear](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_Spear), Newfoundland, at 52°37′W, to [Mount St. Elias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Saint_Elias), Yukon Territory, at 141°W.[^cite_note-p496-127]

Canada can be divided into seven physiographic regions: the [Canadian Shield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_Shield), the [Interior Plains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interior_Plains), the [Great Lakes–St. Lawrence Lowlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Lakes%E2%80%93St._Lawrence_Lowlands), the [Appalachian region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Appalachian_Mountains), the [Western Cordillera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacific_Cordillera_\(Canada\)), [Hudson Bay Lowlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hudson_Bay_Lowlands), and the [Arctic Archipelago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arctic_Archipelago).[^cite_note-128] [Boreal forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiga) prevail throughout the country, ice is prominent in [northern Arctic regions](79987.md) and through the [Rocky Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rocky_Mountains), and the relatively flat [Canadian Prairies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_Prairies) in the southwest facilitate productive agriculture.[^cite_note-McColl2005-121] The Great Lakes feed the [St. Lawrence River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Lawrence_River) (in the southeast) where the lowlands host much of Canada's economic output.[^cite_note-McColl2005-121] Canada has over 2,000,000 lakes—563 of which are larger than 100 square kilometres (39 sq mi)—containing much of the world's [fresh water](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fresh_water).[^cite_note-129] There are also fresh-water glaciers in the [Canadian Rockies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_Rockies), the [Coast Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coast_Mountains), and the [Arctic Cordillera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arctic_Cordillera).[^cite_note-130] [Canada is geologically active](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geology_of_Canada), having [many earthquakes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_earthquakes_in_Canada) and [potentially active volcanoes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volcanism_of_Canada).[^cite_note-131]

### Climate

Main articles: [Temperature in Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temperature_in_Canada) and [Climate change in Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change_in_Canada)



Köppen climate classification types of Canada

Average winter and summer high [temperatures across Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temperature_in_Canada) vary from region to region. Winters can be harsh in many parts of the country, particularly in the interior and Prairie provinces, which experience a [continental climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_climate), where daily average temperatures are near −15 [°C](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celsius) (5 [°F](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fahrenheit)), but can drop below −40 °C (−40 °F) with severe [wind chills](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wind_chill).[^cite_note-132] In non-coastal regions, snow can cover the ground for almost six months of the year, while in parts of the north snow can persist year-round. Coastal British Columbia has a temperate climate, with a mild and rainy winter. On the east and west coasts, average high temperatures are generally in the low 20s °C (70s °F), while between the coasts, the average summer high temperature ranges from 25 to 30 °C (77 to 86 °F), with temperatures in some interior locations occasionally exceeding 40 °C (104 °F).[^cite_note-133]

Much of [Northern Canada](79987.md) is covered by ice and [permafrost](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Permafrost). The future of the permafrost is uncertain because the Arctic has been warming at three times the global average as a result of [climate change in Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change_in_Canada).[^cite_note-134] Canada's annual average temperature over land has risen by 1.7 °C (3.1 °F), with changes ranging from 1.1 to 2.3 °C (2.0 to 4.1 °F) in various regions, since 1948.[^cite_note-McColl2005-121] The rate of warming has been higher across the North and in the Prairies.[^cite_note-ClimateReport-135] In the southern regions of Canada, [air pollution from both Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_pollution_in_Canada) and the United States—caused by metal smelting, burning coal to power utilities, and vehicle emissions—has resulted in [acid rain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acid_rain), which has severely impacted waterways, forest growth, and agricultural productivity.[^cite_note-136] Canada is one of the largest [greenhouse gas emitters globally](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_greenhouse_gas_emissions),[^cite_note-137] with emissions increased by 16.5 percent between 1990 and 2022.[^cite_note-j018-138]

### Biodiversity

Main article: [Wildlife of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wildlife_of_Canada)

Map showing Canada divided into different ecozones

&#x20;[Terrestrial ecozones and ecoprovinces of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecozones_of_Canada). Ecozones are identified with a unique colour. Ecoprovinces are subdivisions of ecozones and are identified with a unique numeric code.

[^cite_note-Statistics_Canada_2018_u350-139]

Canada is divided into [15 terrestrial and five marine ecozones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecozones_of_Canada).[^cite_note-140] These ecozones encompass over 80,000 classified species of [Canadian wildlife](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wildlife_of_Canada), with an equal number yet to be formally recognized or discovered.[^cite_note-141] Although Canada has a low percentage of [endemic species compared to other countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Megadiverse_countries),[^cite_note-142] due to human activities, [invasive species](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wildlife_of_Canada#Invasive_species), and [environmental issues in the country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_issues_in_Canada), there are currently more than [800 species at risk of being lost](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Wildlife_Species_at_Risk_\(Canada\)).[^cite_note-143] About 65 percent of Canada's resident species are considered "Secure".[^cite_note-144] Over half of Canada's landscape is intact and relatively free of human development.[^cite_note-145] The [boreal forest of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boreal_forest_of_Canada) is considered to be the largest [intact forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intact_forest_landscape) on Earth, with approximately 3,000,000 square kilometres (1,200,000 sq mi) undisturbed by roads, cities or industry.[^cite_note-146] Since the end of the last [glacial period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glacial_period), Canada has consisted of [eight distinct forest regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forests_of_Canada#Regions).[^cite_note-147]

Approximately 12.1 percent of the nation's landmass and freshwater are [conservation areas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Wildlife_Area), including 11.4 percent designated as [protected areas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protected_areas_of_Canada).[^cite_note-conserved-148] Approximately 13.8 percent of its territorial waters are conserved, including 8.9 percent designated as protected areas.[^cite_note-conserved-148] Canada's first [National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Parks_of_Canada), [Banff National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banff_National_Park) was established in 1885.[^cite_note-149] Canada's oldest provincial park, [Algonquin Provincial Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algonquin_Provincial_Park) was established in 1893.[^cite_note-150] Established in 2015, [Lake Superior National Marine Conservation Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Superior_National_Marine_Conservation_Area) is the world's largest freshwater protected area.[^cite_note-151] Canada's largest national wildlife region, established in 2018, is the [Scott Islands Marine National Wildlife Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scott_Islands_Marine_National_Wildlife_Area).[^cite_note-152]

## Government and politics

Main articles: [Government of Canada](616255.md) and [Politics of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_of_Canada)



Aerial view of [Canadian Parliament Buildings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_Parliament_Buildings) and their surroundings

Canada is described as a "[full democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democracy_Index)",[^cite_note-f722-153] with a tradition of [liberalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberalism),[^cite_note-154] and an [egalitarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egalitarianism),[^cite_note-155] [moderate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_moderate) political ideology.[^cite_note-156] Since the 1960s, an emphasis on [social justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_justice) has been a distinguishing element of Canada's political culture.[^cite_note-157] [Peace, order, and good government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peace,_order,_and_good_government), alongside an [Implied Bill of Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Implied_Bill_of_Rights), are founding principles of [Canadian federalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_federalism).[^cite_note-158]

At the federal level, Canada has been dominated by two relatively [centrist](39619035.md) parties practising "brokerage politics":[^cite_note-politics-161] the [centre-left](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centre-left_politics) leaning [Liberal Party of Canada](149536.md)[^cite_note-162] and the [centre-right](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centre-right) leaning [Conservative Party of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Party_of_Canada) (or [its predecessors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Party_of_Canada#Predecessors)).[^cite_note-Gill_2021_p._485-163] The historically predominant Liberals position themselves at the centre of the political scale.[^cite_note-Gill_2021_p._485-163] Five parties had representatives elected to Parliament in the [2025 election](68483222.md)—the Liberals, who formed a minority government that transitioned into a majority government in April 2026 owing to [defections from other parties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crossing_the_floor); the Conservatives, who became the [Official Opposition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Official_Opposition_\(Canada\)); the [Bloc Québécois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bloc_Qu%C3%A9b%C3%A9cois); the [New Democratic Party](19283982.md) (occupying the [left](18499.md)[^cite_note-164]); and the [Green Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_Party_of_Canada).[^cite_note-165] [Far-right](221220.md) and [far-left](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Far-left) politics have never been a prominent force in Canadian society.[^cite_note-166]

Canada has a [parliamentary system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_system) within the context of a [constitutional monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_monarchy)—the [monarchy of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monarchy_of_Canada) being the foundation of the executive, [legislative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legislative), and [judicial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judicial) branches.[^cite_note-167] The [reigning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reign) monarch is also monarch of [14 other sovereign Commonwealth countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_realm)[^cite_note-168] and [Canada's 10 provinces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monarchy_in_the_Canadian_provinces). The monarch appoints a representative, the [governor general](38152.md), on the [advice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Advice_\(constitutional_law\)) of the [prime minister](24135.md), to carry out most of their ceremonial royal duties.[^cite_note-169]

King Charles III

[Charles III](125248.md)\
[King](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monarchy_of_Canada)

Governor General Louise Arbour

[Louise Arbour](89846.md)\
[Governor general](38152.md)

Prime Minister Mark Carney

[Mark Carney](13571049.md)\
[Prime minister](24135.md)

The monarchy is the source of [sovereignty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_sovereignty) and authority in Canada.[^cite_note-170] However, while the governor general or monarch may exercise their power without ministerial [advice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Advice_\(constitutional_law\)) in rare [crisis situations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_crisis),[^cite_note-Forseyp1-171] the use of the executive powers (or [royal prerogative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_prerogative)) is otherwise directed by the [Cabinet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cabinet_of_Canada), a committee of [ministers of the Crown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_of_the_Crown) responsible to the elected [House of Commons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Commons_of_Canada) and chosen and headed by the prime minister,[^cite_note-172] the [head of government](37570.md). To ensure the stability of government, the governor general will usually appoint as prime minister the person who is the current leader of the political party that can obtain the confidence of a [majority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majority_rule) of members in the House.[^cite_note-173] The [Prime Minister's Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Office_of_the_Prime_Minister_\(Canada\)) (PMO) is one of the most powerful institutions in government, initiating most legislation for parliamentary approval and selecting for appointment by the Crown the governor general, [lieutenant governors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lieutenant_Governor_\(Canada\)), senators, federal court judges, and heads of [Crown corporations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_corporations_of_Canada) and government agencies.[^cite_note-Forseyp1-171] The leader of the party with the second-most seats usually becomes the [leader of the Official Opposition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leader_of_the_Official_Opposition_\(Canada\)) and is part of an adversarial parliamentary system intended to keep the government in check.[^cite_note-174]

The House of Commons chamber

The [House of Commons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Commons_of_Canada) in its temporary location, the [West Block](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Block)

[^cite_note-Services_Canada_2023_u321-175]

The [Parliament of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_of_Canada) passes all federal statute laws. It comprises the monarch, the House of Commons, and the [Senate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senate_of_Canada). While Canada inherited the British concept of [parliamentary supremacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_supremacy), this was later, with the enactment of the Constitution Act, 1982, all but completely superseded by the American notion of [the supremacy of the law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supremacy_Clause).[^cite_note-176]

Each of the 343 [members of Parliament](66805492.md) in the House of Commons is elected by simple plurality in an [electoral district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_district_\(Canada\)) or riding. The *[Constitution Act, 1982](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_Act,_1982)*, requires that no more than five years pass between elections, although the *[Canada Elections Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canada_Elections_Act)* limits this to four years with a "fixed" election date in October; [general elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elections_in_Canada) still must be called by the governor general and can be triggered by either the advice of the prime minister or a lost [confidence vote](208101.md) in the House.[^cite_note-177] The 105 members of the Senate, whose seats are apportioned on a regional basis, serve until age 75.[^cite_note-178]

[Canadian federalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_federalism) divides government responsibilities between the federal government and the 10 provinces. [Provincial legislatures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legislative_assemblies_of_Canadian_provinces_and_territories) are [unicameral](204304.md) and operate in parliamentary fashion similar to the House of Commons.[^cite_note-Montpetit-179] Canada's three territories also have legislatures, but these are not sovereign, have fewer constitutional responsibilities than the provinces,[^cite_note-180] and differ structurally from their provincial counterparts.[^cite_note-181]

### Law

Main article: [Law of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_of_Canada)

The [Constitution of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Canada) is the supreme law of the country and consists of written text and unwritten conventions.[^cite_note-182] The *Constitution Act, 1867* (known as the [British North America Act, 1867](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_North_America_Acts) prior to 1982), affirmed governance based on parliamentary precedent and divided powers between the federal and provincial governments.[^cite_note-183] The *Statute of Westminster, 1931*, granted full autonomy, and the *Constitution Act, 1982*, ended all legislative ties to Britain, as well as adding a constitutional amending formula and the *[Canadian Charter of Rights and Freedoms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_Charter_of_Rights_and_Freedoms)*.[^cite_note-184] The *Charter* guarantees basic [rights and freedoms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_in_Canada) that usually cannot be overridden by any government; a [notwithstanding clause](123500.md) allows Parliament and the provincial legislatures to override certain sections of the *Charter* for a period of five years.[^cite_note-185]

Supreme Court of Canada building

The [Supreme Court of Canada](123468.md) in Ottawa, west of Parliament Hill

[Canada's judiciary](1124694.md) interprets laws and has the power to strike down acts of Parliament that violate the constitution. The [Supreme Court of Canada](123468.md) is the highest court, final arbiter, and has been led since 2017 by [Richard Wagner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Wagner_\(judge\)), the [Chief Justice of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_Justice_of_Canada).[^cite_note-186] The governor general appoints the court's nine members on the advice of the prime minister and [minister of justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_of_Justice_and_Attorney_General_of_Canada).[^cite_note-UCP-2018-187] The federal Cabinet also appoints justices to superior courts in the provincial and territorial jurisdictions.[^cite_note-188]

[Common law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_of_Canada#Common_law) prevails everywhere except [Quebec, where civil law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quebec_law) predominates.[^cite_note-189] [Criminal law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criminal_law_of_Canada) is solely a federal responsibility and is uniform throughout Canada.[^cite_note-190] [Law enforcement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_enforcement_in_Canada), including criminal courts, is officially a provincial responsibility, conducted by provincial and municipal police forces.[^cite_note-191] In most rural and some urban areas, policing responsibilities are contracted to the federal [Royal Canadian Mounted Police](69080.md).[^cite_note-Sullivan-2005-192]

[Canadian Aboriginal law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_Aboriginal_law) provides certain [constitutionally recognized rights to land](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aboriginal_land_title_in_Canada) and traditional practices for Indigenous groups in Canada.[^cite_note-193] Various treaties and case laws were established to mediate relations between Europeans and many Indigenous peoples.[^cite_note-FN-194] The role of Aboriginal law and the rights they support were reaffirmed by [section 35 of the Constitution Act, 1982](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Section_35_of_the_Constitution_Act,_1982).[^cite_note-FN-194] These rights may include provision of services, such as healthcare through the [Indian Health Transfer Policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Health_Transfer_Policy), and exemption from taxation.[^cite_note-195]

### Provinces and territories

Main article: [Provinces and territories of Canada](75763.md)

See also: [Canadian federalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_federalism)

[Labelled map of Canada detailing its provinces and territories](75763.md)

Political map of Canada showing its [10 provinces and 3 territories](75763.md)

[^cite_note-Anon._y967-196]

Canada is a federation composed of 10 [federated states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federated_states), called provinces, and three [federal territories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_territories). These may be grouped into [four main regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_regions_of_Canada): [Western Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Canada), [Central Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Canada), [Atlantic Canada](71095.md), and [Northern Canada](79987.md) (*[Eastern Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Canada)* refers to Central Canada and Atlantic Canada together).[^cite_note-197] Provinces and territories have responsibility for social programs such as [healthcare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Healthcare_in_Canada), [education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Canada), and [social programs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_programs_in_Canada),[^cite_note-198] as well as administration of justice (but not criminal law). Although the provinces collect more revenue than the federal government, [equalization payments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equalization_payments) are made by the federal government to ensure reasonably uniform standards of services and taxation are kept between the richer and poorer provinces.[^cite_note-199]

The major difference between a Canadian province and a territory is that provinces receive their sovereignty from the Crown[^cite_note-200] and power and authority from the *Constitution Act, 1867*, whereas territorial governments have powers delegated to them by the [Parliament of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_of_Canada)[^cite_note-201] and the commissioners represent the [King in his federal Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King-in-Council),[^cite_note-202] rather than the monarch directly. The powers flowing from the *Constitution Act, 1867*, are divided between the federal government and the provincial governments to exercise exclusively[^cite_note-203] and any changes to that arrangement require a [constitutional amendment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amendments_to_the_Constitution_of_Canada), while changes to the roles and powers of the territories may be performed unilaterally by the Parliament of Canada.[^cite_note-204]

### Foreign relations

Main article: [Foreign relations of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_Canada)



[Diplomatic missions of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diplomatic_missions_of_Canada)

[^cite_note-GAC_2014_c263-205]

&#x20; Countries that host a Canadian Embassy or High Commission

&#x20; Interests section and other representations

&#x20; Countries that do not host Canadian diplomatic missions

Canada is recognized as a [middle power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_power) for its role in global affairs with a tendency to pursue [multilateral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multilateralism) and [international](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internationalism_\(politics\)) solutions.[^cite_note-206] The country actively promotes [its domestically shared values](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_values) of human rights, the [rule of law](25166191.md) and [foreign aid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_foreign_aid) through [membership in multiple international organizations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_organisation_membership_of_Canada).[^cite_note-207]

The "golden age of Canadian diplomacy" refers to a period in Canadian history, typically considered to be the mid-twentieth century, when Canada experienced a high level of success in its foreign relations and diplomatic efforts.[^cite_note-208] Canada's leading role in the creation of the [Universal Declaration of Human Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_Declaration_of_Human_Rights)[^cite_note-209] and [its development of modern peacekeeping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_peacekeeping) during this period played a major role in the country's positive global image.[^cite_note-210] The country has since fostered a [favourable reputation on the world stage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_rankings_of_Canada), widely viewed as a responsible and ethical country.[^cite_note-211]

[Canada and the United States](5199.md) have a long and complex relationship;[^cite_note-212] historically close allies, they have co-operated regularly on military campaigns and humanitarian efforts.[^cite_note-213] Canada also maintains traditional [ties to the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canada%E2%80%93United_Kingdom_relations) and [to France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canada%E2%80%93France_relations),[^cite_note-Morrison_2008_p._177-214] along with both countries' former colonies, through its membership in the [Commonwealth of Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Nations) and the *[Organisation internationale de la Francophonie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organisation_internationale_de_la_Francophonie)*.[^cite_note-215] Canada is noted for having a positive [relationship with the Netherlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canada%E2%80%93Netherlands_relations), owing, in part, to its contribution to the [Dutch liberation during the Second World War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberation_of_the_Netherlands).[^cite_note-netherlands-92] [Canada has diplomatic and consular offices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_diplomatic_missions_of_Canada) in over 270 locations in approximately 180 foreign countries.[^cite_note-GAC_2014_c263-205]

### Military

Main articles: [Canadian Armed Forces](182792.md) and [Canadian peacekeeping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_peacekeeping)

Further information: [Military history of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_history_of_Canada)

A fighter jet taking off from a runway

A Canadian [McDonnell Douglas CF-18 Hornet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McDonnell_Douglas_CF-18_Hornet) in "special markings" used by the 2014 [CF-18 Demonstration Team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CF-18_Demonstration_Team)

[^cite_note-Skies_Mag_2014_p953-216]

Alongside many [domestic obligations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Canadian_military_operations#Domestic), more than 3,000 [Canadian Armed Forces](182792.md) (CAF) personnel are [deployed in multiple foreign military operations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Canadian_military_operations#Foreign).[^cite_note-217] The Canadian unified forces comprise the [Royal Canadian Navy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Canadian_Navy), [Canadian Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_Army), and [Royal Canadian Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Canadian_Air_Force). The nation employs a professional, volunteer force of approximately 68,000 active personnel and 27,000 reserve personnel.[^cite_note-218] Canada will spend 2% of its GDP on defence by the 2026 fiscal year, up from 1.3% and 1.4% in previous years.[^cite_note-y518-219]

Peacekeeping is deeply embedded in Canadian culture and a distinguishing feature that Canadians feel sets their foreign policy apart from the United States.[^cite_note-220] Canada has long been reluctant to participate in military interventions that are not sanctioned by the United Nations,[^cite_note-Massie_2019_pp._575–594-221] such as the [Vietnam War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canada_and_the_Vietnam_War), the [2003 invasion of Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canada_and_the_Iraq_War) and the [2026 Iran war](82593114.md).[^cite_note-Massie_2019_pp._575–594-221] Since the 21st century, Canadian direct participation in UN peacekeeping efforts has greatly declined.[^cite_note-JohnsonJoshee2007-222] The large decrease was a result of Canada directing its participation to UN-sanctioned military [operations through the North Atlantic Treaty Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canada_in_NATO), rather than directly through the UN.[^cite_note-McQuaig2010bt-223] The change to participation via NATO has resulted in a shift towards more militarized and deadly missions rather than traditional peacekeeping duties.[^cite_note-James_Michaud_OReilly_2006_p._177-224]

## Economy

Main article: [Economy of Canada](5195.md)



The [Toronto financial district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Financial_District,_Toronto) is the second-largest financial centre in North America, the seventh-largest globally in employment and the heart of Canada's finance industry.

[^cite_note-225]

Canada's [mixed-market economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mixed-market)[^cite_note-226] is highly [developed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Developed_economy), ranking as the world's [ninth-largest by nominal GDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(nominal\)) as of 2023, at approximately [US\$](18717338.md)2.221 trillion.[^cite_note-227] The country is one of the world's largest [trading nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trading_nation), with a highly [globalized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Globalized) economy.[^cite_note-228] In 2021, Canadian trade in goods and services reached \$2.016 trillion.[^cite_note-econ-229] [Canada's exports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canada's_exports) totalled over \$637 billion, while its imported goods were worth over \$631 billion, of which approximately \$391 billion originated from the United States.[^cite_note-econ-229] The [Toronto Stock Exchange](205584.md) is the ninth-largest stock exchange in the world by [market capitalization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Market_capitalization), listing over 1,500 companies with a combined market capitalization of over US\$2 trillion.[^cite_note-230]

The [Bank of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bank_of_Canada) is the [central bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_bank) of the country.[^cite_note-Watts-1993-231] The [minister of finance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_of_Finance_\(Canada\)) and [minister of innovation, science, and industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_of_Innovation,_Science_and_Industry) use data from [Statistics Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statistics_Canada) to enable financial planning and develop economic policy.[^cite_note-232] Canada has a strong [cooperative banking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cooperative_banking) sector, with the world's highest per-capita membership in [credit unions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Credit_union).[^cite_note-233] Canada "is widely regarded as among the least corrupt countries of the world",[^cite_note-234] with a relatively low level of [income disparity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_inequality).[^cite_note-235] The country's average household [disposable income](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disposable_income) per capita is "well above" the OECD average.[^cite_note-236] In 2024, Canada had the second-largest [foreign direct investment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_direct_investment) stock-to-GDP ratio among G20 countries and the lowest net debt of G7 members.[^cite_note-w479-237] Notwithstanding, [Canada ranks among the lowest of the most advanced economies in several indicators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_rankings_of_Canada#Lowest_rankings_of_advanced_economies), including income growth[^cite_note-h331-238] and [housing affordability](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Affordable_housing_in_Canada).[^cite_note-j722-239]

Since the early 20th century, the growth of [Canada's manufacturing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manufacturing_in_Canada), [mining](65647120.md), and service sectors has transformed the nation from a largely rural economy to an urbanized, industrial one.[^cite_note-240] The Canadian economy is dominated by the [service industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tertiary_sector_of_the_economy), which employs about three-quarters of the country's workforce.[^cite_note-241] Canada has an unusually important [primary sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primary_sector_of_the_economy), of which the [forestry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forestry_in_Canada) and [petroleum industries](4071088.md) are the most prominent components.[^cite_note-SueyoshiGoto2018-242] Many towns in northern Canada, where agriculture is difficult, are sustained by nearby mines or sources of timber.[^cite_note-243]



&#x20; Canada

&#x20; [Countries and territories with free-trade agreements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_trade_agreements_of_Canada)

[^cite_note-GAC_2020-244]

Canada's economic integration with the United States has increased significantly since the [Second World War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_World_War).[^cite_note-245] The [Canada – United States Free Trade Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canada_%E2%80%93_United_States_Free_Trade_Agreement) (FTA) of 1988 eliminated tariffs between the two countries, while the [North American Free Trade Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_American_Free_Trade_Agreement) (NAFTA) expanded the free-trade zone to include [Mexico](3966054.md) in 1994 (later replaced by the [Canada–United States–Mexico Agreement](58620732.md)).[^cite_note-246] As of 2023, [Canada is a signatory to 15 free trade agreements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_trade_agreements_of_Canada) with 51 different countries.[^cite_note-GAC_2020-244]

Canada is one of the few developed nations that are net exporters of energy.[^cite_note-247] [Atlantic Canada possess vast offshore deposits of natural gas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Offshore_drilling_in_Atlantic_Canada),[^cite_note-248] and Alberta hosts the fourth-largest oil reserves in the world.[^cite_note-Monga_2022-249] The vast [Athabasca oil sands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Athabasca_oil_sands) and other oil reserves give Canada 13 percent of global oil reserves, constituting the world's [third- or fourth-largest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_proven_oil_reserves).[^cite_note-250] Canada is additionally one of the world's [largest suppliers of agricultural products](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agriculture_in_Canada); the Canadian Prairies region is one of the most important global producers of wheat, [canola](71016451.md), and other grains.[^cite_note-251] [Canada's main exports](46644724.md) are zinc, uranium, gold, nickel, [platinoids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Platinum_group), aluminum, steel, iron ore, coking coal, lead, copper, [molybdenum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Molybdenum), cobalt, and cadmium.[^cite_note-252] Canada has a sizeable manufacturing sector centred in southern Ontario and Quebec, with [automobiles](23560776.md) and [aeronautics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aeronautics) representing particularly important industries.[^cite_note-253] The [country's fishing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fishing_industry_in_Canada) and [tourism industries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism_in_Canada) are also a key contributor to the economy.[^cite_note-254]

### Science and technology

Main article: [Science and technology in Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Science_and_technology_in_Canada)
