In 2020, Canada spent approximately \$41.9 billion on domestic [research and development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Research_and_development), with supplementary estimates for 2022 at \$43.2 billion.[^cite_note-255] As of 2023, the country has produced 15 [Nobel laureates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Nobel_laureates_by_country) in [physics](52497.md), [chemistry](25416893.md), and [medicine](52502.md).[^cite_note-256] The country [ranks seventh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_number_of_scientific_and_technical_journal_articles) in the worldwide share of articles published in [scientific journals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_journals), according to the [Nature Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nature_Index),[^cite_note-257] and is home to the headquarters of a number of global technology firms.[^cite_note-258] Canada [has one of the highest levels of Internet access in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_number_of_Internet_users),[^cite_note-g345-259] and ranks among the most expensive countries globally for internet and mobile services.[^cite_note-x358-260]



The Canadian-built [Space Shuttle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_Shuttle) robotic arm (left), referred to as [Canadarm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadarm), transferred the [P5 truss segment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Integrated_Truss_Structure) over to the Canadian-built [space station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_station) robotic arm, referred to as [Canadarm2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadarm2).

[^cite_note-Canadian_Space_Agency_2002_a865-261]

[Canada's achievements in science and technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Canadian_inventions,_innovations,_and_discoveries) include the creation of the modern [alkaline battery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alkaline_battery),[^cite_note-262] the [discovery of insulin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Insulin#Discovery),[^cite_note-263] the development of the [polio vaccine](192198.md),[^cite_note-264] and discoveries about the interior structure of the [atomic nucleus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atomic_nucleus).[^cite_note-265] Other major Canadian scientific contributions include the [artificial cardiac pacemaker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artificial_cardiac_pacemaker), mapping the [visual cortex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visual_cortex),[^cite_note-266] the development of the [electron microscope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electron_microscope),[^cite_note-267] [plate tectonics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plate_tectonics), [deep learning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deep_learning), [multi-touch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multi-touch) technology, and the identification of the first [black hole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_hole), [Cygnus X-1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cygnus_X-1).[^cite_note-268] Canada has a long history of discovery in genetics, which include [stem cells](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stem_cell), [site-directed mutagenesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Site-directed_mutagenesis), [T-cell receptor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T-cell_receptor), and the identification of the genes that cause [Fanconi anemia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fanconi_anemia), [cystic fibrosis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cystic_fibrosis), and [early-onset Alzheimer's disease](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early-onset_Alzheimer's_disease), among numerous other diseases.[^cite_note-269]

The [Canadian Space Agency](328618.md) runs an active space program focused on deep-space, planetary, and aviation research, along with rockets and satellites.[^cite_note-270] Canada launched its first satellite, [Alouette 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alouette_1), in 1962.[^cite_note-271] It contributes to the [International Space Station](15043.md) and is known for its robotic tools, such as multiple [Canadarms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadarm).[^cite_note-272] Canada has initiated many long-term projects, including the [Radarsat satellite series](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radarsat) and the [Black Brant rocket series](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Brant_\(rocket\)).[^cite_note-273]

## Demographics

Main articles: [Demographics of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Canada), [List of cities in Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_in_Canada), and [List of census metropolitan areas and agglomerations in Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_census_metropolitan_areas_and_agglomerations_in_Canada)



[Canada population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Population_of_Canada) density map (2014)\
Top left: The [Quebec City–Windsor Corridor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quebec_City%E2%80%93Windsor_Corridor) is the most densely inhabited and heavily industrialized region.

[^cite_note-Statistics_Canada_2015_l621-274]

The [2021 Canadian census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Canadian_census) enumerated [a total population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Population_of_Canada_by_year) of 36,991,981, an increase of around 5.2 percent over the 2016 figure.[^cite_note-2021cen-275] It is estimated that Canada's population surpassed 40,000,000 in 2023.[^cite_note-Statistics_Canada_2023_e538-276] The main drivers of population growth are [immigration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_to_Canada) and, to a lesser extent, natural growth.[^cite_note-277] Canada has one of the highest per-capita immigration rates in the world,[^cite_note-278] driven mainly by [economic policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_impact_of_immigration_to_Canada) and [family reunification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_categories_\(Canada\)).[^cite_note-279] A record 483,390 immigrants were admitted in 2024.[^cite_note-280] Canada leads the world in [refugee resettlement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_country_resettlement); it resettled more than 47,600 in 2022.[^cite_note-g225-281] New immigrants settle mostly in [major urban areas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_the_largest_population_centres_in_Canada), such as Toronto, Montreal, and Vancouver.[^cite_note-282]

Canada's population density, at 4.2 inhabitants per square kilometre (11/sq mi), is among the lowest in the world,[^cite_note-2021cen-275] with approximately 95 percent of the population residing south of the [55th parallel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/55th_parallel_north) north.[^cite_note-OECD2014-283] About 80 percent of the population lives within 150 kilometres (93 mi) of the border with the contiguous United States.[^cite_note-284] Canada is highly urbanized, with over 80 percent of the population living in urban centres.[^cite_note-World_Bank_Open_Data_s787-285] The majority of Canadians (over 70%) live south of the [49th parallel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/49th_parallel_north), with 50 percent of Canadians living south of 45°42′ (45.7 degrees) north.[^cite_note-a715-286] The most densely populated part of the country is the [Quebec City–Windsor Corridor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quebec_City%E2%80%93Windsor_Corridor) in Southern Quebec and Southern Ontario along the Great Lakes and the St. Lawrence River.[^cite_note-McMurryShepherd2004-287]

The majority of Canadians (81.1%) live in family households, 12.1 percent report living alone, and 6.8 percent live with other relatives or unrelated persons.[^cite_note-\:0-288] Households comprising couples with or without children make up 51 percent of all households; 8.7 percent of households are single-parent households, 2.9 percent are multigenerational households, and 29.3 percent are single-person households.[^cite_note-\:0-288]&#x20;

### Ethnicity

Main article: [Ethnic origins of people in Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_origins_of_people_in_Canada)

Respondents in the [2021 Canadian census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Canadian_census) self-reported over 450 "[ethnic or cultural origins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_origins_of_people_in_Canada)".[^cite_note-2021newsrelease-290] The major [panethnic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panethnicity) groups chosen were: [European](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_groups_in_Europe) (52.5%), [North American](21139.md) (22.9%), [Asian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_groups_in_Asia) (19.3%), [North American Indigenous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_of_the_Americas) (6.1%), [African](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_ethnic_groups_of_Africa) (3.8%), [Latin, Central and South American](18524.md) (2.5%), [Caribbean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caribbean_people) (2.1%), [Oceanian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oceanian_people) (0.3%), and other (6%).[^cite_note-291] Over 60 percent of Canadians reported a single origin, and 36 percent reported having multiple ethnic origins, thus the overall total is greater than 100 percent.[^cite_note-2021newsrelease-290]



The top 168 [ethnic or cultural origins self-reported by Canadians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_origins_of_people_in_Canada) in the 2021 census

[^cite_note-Statistics_Canada_u055-292]

The country's ten largest self-reported ethnic or cultural origins in 2021 were [Canadian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_ethnicity)[^cite_note-Canadian-295] (accounting for 15.6 percent of the population), followed by [English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_Canadians) (14.7%), [Irish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish_Canadians) (12.1%), [Scottish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Canadians) (12.1%), [French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Canadians) (11.0%), [German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Canadians) (8.1%), [Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Canadians) (4.7%), [Italian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_Canadians) (4.3%), [Indian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Canadians) (3.7%), and [Ukrainian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_Canadians) (3.5%).[^cite_note-ethnicpopulationordered2021-296]

Of the 36.3 million people enumerated in 2021, approximately 25.4 million reported being "[White](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_people)", representing 69.8 percent of the population.[^cite_note-2021newsrelease-290] The Indigenous population representing 5 percent or 1.8 million people, grew by 9.4 percent compared to the non-Indigenous population, which grew by 5.3 percent from 2016 to 2021.[^cite_note-Statistics_Canada_2022a-297] One out of every four Canadians or 26.5 percent of the population belonged to a non-White and non-Indigenous [visible minority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visible_minority),[^cite_note-298][^cite_note-300] the largest of which in 2021 were [South Asian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Asian_Canadians) (2.6 million people; 7.1%), Chinese (1.7 million; 4.7%), [Black](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Canadians) (1.5 million; 4.3%), [Filipinos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filipino_Canadians) (960,000 2.6%), [Arabs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Canadians) (690,000; 1.9%), Latin Americans (580,000; 1.6%), [Southeast Asians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southeast_Asian_Canadians) (390,000; 1.1%), [West Asians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Asian_Canadians) (360,000; 1.0%), [Koreans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_Canadians) (220,000; 0.6%) and [Japanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_Canadians) (99,000; 0.3%).[^cite_note-2021newsrelease-290]

Between 2011 and 2016, the visible minority population rose by 18.4 percent.[^cite_note-301] In 1961, about 300,000 people, less than two percent of Canada's population, were members of visible minority groups.[^cite_note-302] The 2021 census indicated that 8.3 million people, or almost one-quarter (23.0%) of the population, reported themselves as being or having been a [landed immigrant or permanent resident](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Permanent_residency_in_Canada) in Canada—above the [1921 census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1921_Canadian_census) previous record of 22.3 percent.[^cite_note-Statistics_Canada_2022b-303] In 2021, India, China, and the Philippines were the top three countries of origin for immigrants moving to Canada.[^cite_note-304]

### Languages

Main article: [Languages of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_Canada)



Percentage of the population who could conduct a conversation in English and French in 2021

[^cite_note-f185-305]

A multitude of languages are used by Canadians, with [English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_English) and [French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_French) (the [official languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Official_language)) being the [mother tongues](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_language) of approximately 54 percent and 19 percent of Canadians, respectively.[^cite_note-\:0-288] [Canada's official bilingualism policies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Official_bilingualism_in_Canada) give citizens the right to receive federal government services in either English or French with official-[language minorities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minority_language) guaranteed their own schools in all provinces and territories.[^cite_note-306]

Quebec's 1974 *[Official Language Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Official_Language_Act_\(Quebec\))* established French as the only official language of the province.[^cite_note-307] Although more than 82 percent of French-speaking Canadians live in Quebec, there are substantial [Francophone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francophone) populations in [New Brunswick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_New_Brunswick), [Alberta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franco-Albertans), and [Manitoba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franco-Manitoban), with [Ontario](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franco-Ontarian) having the largest French-speaking population outside Quebec.[^cite_note-308] New Brunswick, the only officially bilingual province, has an [Acadian French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acadian_French) minority constituting 33 percent of the population.[^cite_note-309] There are also clusters of Acadians in southwestern Nova Scotia, on Cape Breton Island, and in central and western Prince Edward Island.[^cite_note-310]

Other provinces have no official languages as such, but French is used as a language of instruction, in courts, and for other government services, in addition to English. Manitoba, Ontario, and Quebec allow for both English and French to be spoken in the provincial legislatures and laws are enacted in both languages. In Ontario, French has some legal status, but is not fully co-official.[^cite_note-311] There are 11 [Indigenous language groups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_Canada), composed of more than 65 distinct languages and dialects.[^cite_note-312] Several Indigenous languages have official status in the Northwest Territories.[^cite_note-313] [Inuktitut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inuktitut) is the majority language in Nunavut and is one of three official languages in the territory.[^cite_note-314]

As of the 2021 census, just over 7.8 million Canadians listed a non-official language as their [first language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_language). Some of the most common non-official first languages include [Mandarin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandarin_Chinese) (679,255 first-language speakers), [Punjabi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjabi_language) (666,585), [Cantonese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cantonese) (553,380), Spanish (538,870), [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic) (508,410), [Tagalog](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tagalog_language) (461,150), Italian (319,505), German (272,865), and [Tamil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tamil_language) (237,890).[^cite_note-\:0-288] The country is also home to many [sign languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sign_language), some of which are Indigenous.[^cite_note-315] [American Sign Language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Sign_Language) (ASL) is used across the country due to the prevalence of ASL in primary and secondary schools.[^cite_note-316] [Quebec Sign Language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quebec_Sign_Language) (LSQ) is used primarily in Quebec.[^cite_note-317]

### Religion

Main article: [Religion in Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Canada)



[Freedom of religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_of_religion_in_Canada) sculpture by Marlene Hilton Moore at the McMurtry Gardens of Justice in [Toronto](64646.md)

[^cite_note-McMurtry_Gardens_of_Justice-318]

Canada is religiously diverse, encompassing a wide range of beliefs and customs.[^cite_note-Cornelissen_2021-319] The [Constitution of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Canada) refers to God; however, Canada has no official church and the government is officially committed to [religious pluralism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_pluralism).[^cite_note-320] [Freedom of religion in Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_of_religion_in_Canada) is a constitutionally protected right.[^cite_note-321]

Rates of religious adherence have steadily decreased since the 1970s.[^cite_note-Cornelissen_2021-319] With Christianity in decline after having once been central and integral to Canadian culture and daily life,[^cite_note-Roberts2005w-322] Canada has become a [post-Christian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Postchristianity), [secular](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secular) state.[^cite_note-323] Although the majority of Canadians consider [religion to be unimportant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Importance_of_religion_by_country) in their daily lives,[^cite_note-Punnett2015-324] they still believe in God.[^cite_note-Haskell2009-325] The practice of religion is generally considered a private matter.[^cite_note-BoyleSheen2013-326]

According to the 2021 census, [Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_Canada) is the largest religion in Canada, with [Roman Catholics](606848.md) representing 29.9 percent of the population having the most adherents. [Christians](18337522.md) overall representing 53.3 percent of the population,[^cite_note-religion-329] are followed by people reporting [irreligion or having no religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irreligion_in_Canada) at 34.6 percent. Other faiths include [Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Canada) (4.9%), [Hinduism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism_in_Canada) (2.3%), [Sikhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikhism_in_Canada) (2.1%), [Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism_in_Canada) (1.0%), [Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judaism_in_Canada) (0.9%), and [Indigenous spirituality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mythologies_of_the_indigenous_peoples_of_the_Americas) (0.2%).[^cite_note-religion2021-327] Canada has the [second-largest national Sikh population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikhism_by_country), behind [India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikhism_in_India).[^cite_note-330]

### Health

Main article: [Healthcare in Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Healthcare_in_Canada)

Healthcare in Canada is delivered through the provincial and territorial systems of [publicly funded health care](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Publicly_funded_health_care), informally called [Medicare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicare_\(Canada\)).[^cite_note-331] It is guided by the provisions of the *[Canada Health Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canada_Health_Act)* of 1984[^cite_note-332] and is [universal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_health_care).[^cite_note-333] Universal access to publicly funded health services "is often considered by Canadians as a fundamental value that ensures national healthcare insurance for everyone wherever they live in the country".[^cite_note-334] Around 30 percent of Canadians' healthcare is paid for through the private sector.[^cite_note-Kroll2012-335] This mostly pays for services not covered or partially covered by Medicare, such as [prescription drugs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prescription_drug), [dentistry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dentistry) and [optometry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Optometry).[^cite_note-Kroll2012-335] Approximately 65 to 75 percent of Canadians have some form of supplementary health insurance; many receive it through their employers or access secondary social service programs.[^cite_note-336]

graph of expenditures as described in the caption

[Health expenditure and financing by country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_total_health_expenditure_per_capita). Total health expenditure per capita in US dollars (PPP).

In common with many other developed countries, Canada is experiencing an increase in healthcare expenditures due to a [demographic shift](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographic_transition) toward an older population, with more retirees and fewer people of working age. In 2021, the average age in Canada was 41.9 years.[^cite_note-\:0-288] Life expectancy is 81.1 years.[^cite_note-337] A 2016 report by the [chief public health officer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_Public_Health_Officer_of_Canada) found that 88 percent of Canadians, one of the highest proportions of the population among G7 countries, indicated that they "had good or very good health".[^cite_note-338] Eighty percent of Canadian adults self-report having at least one major risk factor for chronic disease: smoking, physical inactivity, unhealthy eating or excessive alcohol use.[^cite_note-GregoryStephens2019-339] Canada has one of the highest rates of adult obesity among [OECD](33853117.md) countries, contributing to approximately 2.7 million cases of [diabetes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diabetes).[^cite_note-GregoryStephens2019-339] Four chronic diseases—[cancer](105219.md) (leading cause of death), [cardiovascular diseases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cardiovascular_diseases), [respiratory diseases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Respiratory_diseases), and diabetes—account for 65 percent of deaths in Canada.[^cite_note-340] There are approximately 8 million people aged 15 and older with one or more [disabilities in Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disability_in_Canada).[^cite_note-k471-341]

In 2024, the [Canadian Institute for Health Information](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_Institute_for_Health_Information) estimated that healthcare spending reached \$372 billion, or 12.4 percent of Canada's GDP for that year.[^cite_note-342] In 2022, Canada's per-capita spending on health expenditures ranked 12th among [health-care systems in the OECD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_total_health_expenditure_per_capita).[^cite_note-theOECD_h799-343] The [Commonwealth Fund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_Fund)'s 2021 report comparing the healthcare systems of the 11 most [developed countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Developed_countries) ranked Canada second-to-last.[^cite_note-commonwealthfund-344] Identified weaknesses were comparatively higher infant mortality rate, the prevalence of chronic conditions, long wait times, poor availability of after-hours care, and a lack of prescription drugs and dental coverage.[^cite_note-commonwealthfund-344] An increasing problem in Canada's health system is a lack of healthcare professionals[^cite_note-Duong_Vogel_2023_pp._E309–E310-345] and hospital capacity.[^cite_note-Thiessen_Partner_Organization_Ma_2023_j399-346]

### Education

Main articles: [Education in Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Canada) and [Higher education in Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Higher_education_in_Canada)



&#x20;Canada by province and territory, showing the percentage of the population aged 25 to 64 who had a bachelor's degree or higher, and the percentage point change from 2016 to 2021

[^cite_note-Statistics_Canada_2022_i032-347]

Education in Canada is for the most part [provided publicly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_school), funded and overseen by [federal](616255.md), [provincial](75763.md), and [local governments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_government).[^cite_note-348] Education is within provincial jurisdiction and a province's curriculum is overseen by its government.[^cite_note-349] Education in Canada is generally divided into [primary education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primary_education), followed by [secondary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secondary_education) and [post-secondary education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tertiary_education). Education in both English and French is available in most places across Canada.[^cite_note-350] Canada has a large number of universities, almost all of which are publicly funded.[^cite_note-351] Established in 1663, [Université Laval](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universit%C3%A9_Laval) is the oldest post-secondary institution in Canada.[^cite_note-BlakeKeshen2017p249-352] The nation's three top ranking universities are the [University of Toronto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Toronto), [McGill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McGill_University), and the [University of British Columbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_British_Columbia).[^cite_note-353] The largest university is the University of Toronto, which has over 85,000 students.[^cite_note-354]

According to a 2022 report by the OECD, Canada is one of the most educated countries in the world;[^cite_note-355] the country ranks first worldwide in the percentage of adults having tertiary education, with over 56 percent of Canadian adults having attained at least an undergraduate college or university degree.[^cite_note-Education_c106-356] Canada spends an average of 5.3 percent of its GDP on education.[^cite_note-TheGlobalEconomy.com_1971_w626-357] The country invests heavily in tertiary education (more than US\$20,000 per student).[^cite_note-358] As of 2022, 89 percent of adults aged 25 to 64 have earned the equivalent of a high-school degree, compared to an OECD average of 75 percent.[^cite_note-OECD_2023_t514-359]

## Culture

Main article: [Culture of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Canada)

Further information: [Canadian values](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_values) and [Canadian identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_identity)



*[Monument to Multiculturalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monument_to_Multiculturalism)* by Francesco Pirelli, in Toronto

[^cite_note-Kuitenbrouwer_2010_g035-360]

Canada is influenced by [British](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_the_United_Kingdom), [French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_culture) and Indigenous cultures and traditions. During the 20th century, Canadians with African, Caribbean, and Asian heritages have added to [Canadian identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_identity).[^cite_note-361] Canada's culture draws influences from its broad range of constituent nationalities, and policies that promote a [just society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Just_society) are constitutionally protected.[^cite_note-362] Since the 1960s, Canada has emphasized [human rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_in_Canada) and inclusiveness for all its people.[^cite_note-363] Canadian identity shifted from primarily British-based to multicultural between the 1960s and 1970s.[^cite_note-d827-364]

[The official state policy of multiculturalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multiculturalism_in_Canada) is often cited as one of Canada's significant accomplishments[^cite_note-365] and a key distinguishing element of Canadian identity.[^cite_note-366] In Quebec, cultural identity is strong and there is a [French Canadian culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Quebec) that is distinct from [English Canadian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_Canada) culture.[^cite_note-367] As a whole, Canada is in theory a [cultural mosaic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_mosaic) of regional ethnic subcultures with diverse areas and [ethnic enclaves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multiculturalism_in_Canada#Settlement).[^cite_note-368]

Canada's approach to governance emphasizing multiculturalism, which is based on selective [immigration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_migrant), [social integration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_integration), and [suppression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissent) of far-right politics, has wide public support.[^cite_note-p671-369] Government policies such as publicly funded health care, [higher taxation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Income_taxes_in_Canada) to redistribute wealth, the [outlawing of capital punishment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_punishment_in_Canada), strong efforts to [eliminate poverty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poverty_in_Canada), [strict gun control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gun_politics_in_Canada), a [social liberal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_liberal) attitude toward [women's rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feminism_in_Canada) (like [pregnancy termination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abortion_in_Canada)) and [LGBT rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT_rights_in_Canada), and legalized [euthanasia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euthanasia_in_Canada) and [cannabis use](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cannabis_in_Canada) are indicators of Canada's political and cultural values.[^cite_note-370] Canadians also identify with the country's foreign aid policies, peacekeeping roles, the [national park system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Parks_of_Canada), and the Canadian Charter of Rights and Freedoms.[^cite_note-371]

### Symbols

Main articles: [National symbols of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_symbols_of_Canada) and [Canadian royal symbols](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_royal_symbols)

The Mother Beaver sculpture outside the House of Commons

*The Mother Beaver* on the Canadian parliament's [Peace Tower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peace_Tower).

[^cite_note-372]

&#x20;The five flowers on the shield each represent an ethnicity—[Tudor rose](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tudor_rose): [English](18803164.md); [fleur de lis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fleur_de_lis): [French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_people); [thistle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thistle): [Scottish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_people); [shamrock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shamrock): [Irish](775859.md); and [leek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leek): [Welsh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welsh_people).

Themes of nature, pioneers, trappers, and traders played an important part in the early development of Canadian symbolism.[^cite_note-373] Modern symbols emphasize the country's geography, northern climate, lifestyles, and the Canadianization of traditional European and Indigenous symbols.[^cite_note-374] The use of the [maple leaf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maple_leaf) as a symbol dates to the early 18th century in New France.[^cite_note-g606-375] The maple leaf is depicted on Canada's [current](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flag_of_Canada) and [previous flags](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_Red_Ensign) and on the [arms of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arms_of_Canada).[^cite_note-symbol1-376] Canada's official tartan, known as the "[maple leaf tartan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regional_tartans_of_Canada)", reflects the colours of the maple leaf through the seasons—green in the [spring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spring_\(season\)), gold in the early [autumn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autumn), red at the first [frost](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frost), and brown after falling.[^cite_note-377] The arms of Canada are closely modelled after [those of the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_coat_of_arms_of_the_United_Kingdom), with French and distinctive Canadian elements replacing or added to those derived from the British version.[^cite_note-378]

Other prominent symbols include the national motto, "*[A mari usque ad mare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A_mari_usque_ad_mare)*" ("from sea to sea"),[^cite_note-379] the sports of [ice hockey](14790.md) and [lacrosse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lacrosse), the [beaver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beaver), [Canada goose](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canada_goose), [common loon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_loon), [Canadian horse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_horse), the Royal Canadian Mounted Police, the Canadian Rockies,[^cite_note-symbol1-376] and, more recently, the Indigenous [totem pole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Totem_pole) and [Inuksuk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inuksuk).[^cite_note-Nels-380] [Canadian cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_cuisine) items such as [Canadian beer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_beer), [maple syrup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maple_syrup), [Nanaimo bars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nanaimo_bar), [butter tarts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Butter_tart), and the [Quebec dishes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuisine_of_Quebec) of [poutine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poutine) and [tourtière](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourti%C3%A8re), alongside material items such as [tuques](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tuque), [canoes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canoes) and [Hudson's Bay point blanket](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hudson's_Bay_point_blanket) are considered as uniquely Canadian.[^cite_note-381] Canadian coins feature many of these symbols: the loon on the [\$1 coin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loonie), the coat of arms on the [50¢ piece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/50-cent_piece_\(Canadian_coin\)), and the beaver on the [nickel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nickel_\(Canadian_coin\)).[^cite_note-Berman2008-382] An image of the monarch appears on [\$20 bank notes](101846.md) and the obverse of coins.[^cite_note-Berman2008-382]

### Literature

Main article: [Canadian literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_literature)



Author [Margaret Atwood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Margaret_Atwood) has suggested that during the 1970s Canadian literature was still looking for a national identity.

[^cite_note-n066-383]

Canadian literature is often divided into French- and English-language literatures, which are rooted in the literary traditions of France and Britain, respectively.[^cite_note-384] The earliest Canadian narratives were of travel and exploration.[^cite_note-385] This developed into three major themes of historical Canadian literature: nature, frontier life, and Canada's position within the world, all of which tie into the [garrison mentality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garrison_mentality).[^cite_note-386] The evolution of Canadian literature is intricately linked to the country's historical and social contexts, often mirroring the challenges and changes in Canadian society.[^cite_note-u778-387] As Canadian literature progressed into the 20th and 21st centuries, it began to address a broader array of subjects and themes, such as women's rights, LGBTQ rights, immigrant experiences, environmental issues, the relationship with Indigenous peoples, and Canadian values and identity.[^cite_note-h220-388]

Financial support from governmental bodies, such as the [Canada Council for the Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canada_Council_for_the_Arts) and various provincial grant programs, facilitates the creation, publication, and promotion of works by [Canadian authors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Canadian_writers).[^cite_note-p619-389] Numerous [Canadian authors have received international literary awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_literature#Canadian_authors_who_have_won_international_awards) including the [Nobel Prize in Literature](23385442.md), the [Booker Prize](4446.md), and the [Pulitzer Prize for Fiction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pulitzer_Prize_for_Fiction).[^cite_note-390] [Canadian literary awards and prizes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_literature#Awards) include the [Governor General's Literary Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor_General's_Literary_Awards), the [Giller Prize](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giller_Prize), the [Latner Griffin Writers' Trust Poetry Prize](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latner_Griffin_Writers'_Trust_Poetry_Prize), the [Burt Award for First Nations, Inuit and Métis Literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burt_Award_for_First_Nations,_Inuit_and_M%C3%A9tis_Literature) and several accolades for literature aimed at children.[^cite_note-i923-391]

### Visual arts

Main article: [Canadian art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_art)

Oil on canvas painting of a tree dominating its rocky landscape during a sunset

*[The Jack Pine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Jack_Pine)* by [Tom Thomson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tom_Thomson). Oil on canvas, 1916, in the collection of the [National Gallery of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Gallery_of_Canada).

[^cite_note-Art_Canada_Institute_-_Institut_de_l'art_canadien_h165-392]

Art in Canada is marked by thousands of years of habitation by Indigenous peoples,[^cite_note-393] and, in later times, artists have combined British, French, Indigenous, and American artistic traditions, at times embracing European styles while working to promote nationalism.[^cite_note-394] The nature of Canadian art reflects these diverse origins, as artists have taken their traditions and adapted these influences to reflect the reality of their lives in Canada.[^cite_note-395] The [Group of Seven](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Group_of_Seven_\(artists\)) is often considered the first uniquely Canadian artistic group and style of painting.[^cite_note-Jessup2001-396] [Inuit art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inuit_art) since the 1950s has been the traditional gift given to foreign dignitaries by the Canadian government.[^cite_note-Stern2010-397]

Historically, the Catholic Church was the primary patron of art in early Canada, especially Quebec.[^cite_note-j452-398] The Government of Canada has played a role in the development of art, through the department of [Canadian Heritage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_Heritage) by giving grants to art galleries,[^cite_note-399] as well as by establishing and funding art schools and colleges across the country, and through the Canada Council for the Arts.[^cite_note-e229-400] The Canada Council [Art Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_Bank) also helps artists by buying and publicizing their work.[^cite_note-j314-401] Great achievements in art in Canada are recognized through [various awards and prizes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Canadian_art_awards), such as the [Molson Prize](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Molson_Prize), the [Audain Prize for the Visual Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Audain_Prize_for_the_Visual_Arts), and the [Governor General's Visual and Media Arts Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_recipients_of_the_Governor_General's_Award_in_Visual_and_Media_Arts).[^cite_note-n795-402]

### Music

Main article: [Music of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Canada)



Original publication of "[O Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/O_Canada)" in English, 1908 (French, 1880)

[^cite_note-The_Canadian_Encyclopedia_2018_g033-403]

Canadian music reflects a [variety of regional scenes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Canadian_cultures).[^cite_note-404] Canada has developed a vast music infrastructure that includes [church halls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_hall), [chamber halls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chamber_music), [conservatories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_school), [academies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academies), [performing arts centres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Performing_arts_center), [record companies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Record_company), [radio stations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_stations), [music charts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_music_charts), and television [music video](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_video) channels.[^cite_note-405] Government support programs, such as the Canada Music Fund, assist a wide range of musicians and entrepreneurs who create, produce and market original and diverse Canadian music.[^cite_note-406] As a result of its cultural importance, as well as government initiatives and regulations, the Canadian music industry is one of the largest in the world,[^cite_note-407] producing internationally renowned [composers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Canadian_composers), [musicians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Canadian_musicians), and [ensembles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_bands_from_Canada).[^cite_note-408] Achievements in music are recognized through various awards and prizes,[^cite_note-j032-409] most notably, the [Juno Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juno_Award) presented by the [Canadian Academy of Recording Arts and Sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_Academy_of_Recording_Arts_and_Sciences).[^cite_note-Edwardson,_Ryan-410] The country also hosts numerous music halls of fame with the [Canadian Music Hall of Fame](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_Music_Hall_of_Fame) honouring musicians at the national level for their lifetime achievements.[^cite_note-411]

"[God Save the King](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God_Save_the_King)" has been used in Canada since the late 1700s and is the country's de facto royal anthem.[^cite_note-t663-412] [Patriotic music by Canadians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthems_and_nationalistic_songs_of_Canada) dates back over 200 years, with "[The Bold Canadian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Bold_Canadian)", written in 1812, popular throughout the 19th century.[^cite_note-413] "[The Maple Leaf Forever](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Maple_Leaf_Forever)", written in 1866, was popular and served as an unofficial national anthem of English Canada.[^cite_note-414] "[O Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/O_Canada)", originally composed in French in 1880, also served as an unofficial national anthem during the 20th century and was adopted as the country's official anthem in 1980.[^cite_note-The_Canadian_Encyclopedia_2018_g033-403]

### Media

Main articles: [Media of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_of_Canada) and [Censorship in Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Censorship_in_Canada)



A [Canadian Broadcasting Corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_Broadcasting_Corporation) (CBC) satellite truck, used for live television broadcasts

Canada's media is [highly autonomous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Press_Freedom_Index), [diverse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multicultural_media_in_Canada), and very regionalized.[^cite_note-415] The *[Broadcasting Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Broadcasting_Act_\(1991\))* declares "the system should serve to safeguard, enrich, and strengthen the cultural, political, social, and economic fabric of Canada".[^cite_note-416] Canada has a well-developed media sector, but its cultural output—particularly in [English films](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_Canada), [television shows](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Television_in_Canada), and [magazines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Canadian_magazines)—is often overshadowed by imports from the United States.[^cite_note-417] As a result, the preservation of a distinctly Canadian culture is supported by federal government programs, laws, and institutions such as the [Canadian Broadcasting Corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_Broadcasting_Corporation) (CBC), the [National Film Board of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Film_Board_of_Canada) (NFB), and the [Canadian Radio-television and Telecommunications Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_Radio-television_and_Telecommunications_Commission) (CRTC).[^cite_note-Edwardson,_Ryan-410]<sup>: [59](https://archive.org/details/canadiancontentc0000edwa/page/59) </sup>

[Canadian mass media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_media_in_Canada), both [print](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newspapers_of_Canada) and [digital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_online_media), and in both official languages, is largely dominated by a "[handful of corporations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_ownership_in_Canada)".[^cite_note-TarasBakardjievaPannekoek20072-418] The largest of these corporations is the country's national [public broadcaster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_broadcaster), the Canadian Broadcasting Corporation, which also plays a significant role in producing domestic cultural content, operating [its own radio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CBC_Radio) and [TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CBC_Television) networks in both English and French.[^cite_note-TarasBakardjievaPannekoek2007-419] In addition to the CBC, some provincial governments offer their own public educational TV broadcast services as well, such as [TVOntario](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TVOntario) and [Télé-Québec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T%C3%A9l%C3%A9-Qu%C3%A9bec).[^cite_note-420]

Non-news media content in Canada, including film and television, is influenced both by local creators as well as by imports from the United States, the United Kingdom, Australia, and France.[^cite_note-421] In an effort to reduce the amount of foreign-made media, government interventions in television broadcasting can include both regulation of content and public financing.[^cite_note-422] [Canadian tax laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taxation_in_Canada) limit foreign competition in magazine advertising.[^cite_note-423]

### Sports

Main article: [Sports in Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sports_in_Canada)



&#x20;*Our Game* sculpture by [Edie Parker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edie_Parker_\(artist\)) outside the [Hockey Hall of Fame](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hockey_Hall_of_Fame) in Toronto

[^cite_note-k228-424]

Canada's official national sports are [ice hockey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ice_hockey_in_Canada) and [lacrosse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lacrosse_in_Canada).[^cite_note-425] Other major professional games include [curling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Curling), [basketball](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basketball_in_Canada), [baseball](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baseball_in_Canada), [soccer](579183.md), and [football](6963.md).[^cite_note-426] Great achievements in Canadian sports are recognized by numerous "Halls of Fame" and museums, such as [Canada's Sports Hall of Fame](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canada's_Sports_Hall_of_Fame).[^cite_note-Danilov1997cv-427]

Canada shares several [major professional sports leagues](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Major_professional_sports_leagues_in_the_United_States_and_Canada) with the United States.[^cite_note-428] Canadian teams in these leagues include seven franchises in the [National Hockey League](21809.md), three [Major League Soccer](579183.md#Major_League_Soccer) teams, and one team in each of [Major League Baseball](38776.md) and the [National Basketball Association](22093.md). Other popular professional competitions include the [Canadian Football League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_Football_League), [National Lacrosse League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Lacrosse_League), the [Canadian Premier League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_Premier_League), and the curling tournaments hosted by [Curling Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Curling_Canada).[^cite_note-429] Canadians identified hockey as their preferred sport for viewing, followed by soccer and then basketball.[^cite_note-n113-430]

In terms of participation, [swimming](27999.md) was the most commonly reported sport by over one-third (35%) of Canadians in 2023.[^cite_note-h903-431] This was closely followed by [cycling](5931.md) (33%) and [running](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Running) (27%).[^cite_note-h903-431] The popularity of specific sports varies;[^cite_note-432] in general, the Canadian-born population was more likely to have participated in [winter sports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winter_sports) such as ice hockey (the most popular young adult team sport),[^cite_note-h903-431] [skating](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ice_skating), [skiing](28478.md) and [snowboarding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Snowboarding), compared with immigrants, who were more likely to have played soccer (the most popular youth team sport),[^cite_note-a691-433] [tennis](29773.md) or basketball.[^cite_note-h903-431] Sports such as [golf](19568112.md), [volleyball](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volleyball_in_Canada), [badminton](3956.md), [bowling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bowling), and [martial arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martial_arts) are also widely enjoyed at the youth and amateur levels.[^cite_note-434]

The [Canada Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canada_Games) is the country's highest-level youth multi-sport event held every two years.[^cite_note-c744-435] Canada has enjoyed success both [at the Winter Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canada_at_the_Winter_Olympics) and [the Summer Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canada_at_the_Summer_Olympics)[^cite_note-436]—particularly the Winter Games as a "winter sports nation".[^cite_note-a796-437] Canada has hosted high-profile international sporting events such as the [1976 Summer Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1976_Summer_Olympics),[^cite_note-438] the [1988 Winter Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1988_Winter_Olympics),[^cite_note-439] the [2010 Winter Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_Winter_Olympics),[^cite_note-440] multiple [Commonwealth Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canada_at_the_Commonwealth_Games),[^cite_note-t655-441] the [2015 FIFA Women's World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_FIFA_Women's_World_Cup),[^cite_note-442] the [2015 Pan American Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_Pan_American_Games) and [2015 Parapan American Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_Parapan_American_Games).[^cite_note-443] The country co-hosted the [2026 FIFA World Cup](36581929.md) alongside Mexico and the United States.[^cite_note-444]

[^cite_note-politics-161]: &#x20;"Brokerage politics: A Canadian term for successful [big tent parties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_tent) that embody a [pluralistic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pluralism_\(political_philosophy\)) catch-all approach to appeal to the median Canadian voter ... adopting [centrist policies](39619035.md) and [electoral coalitions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_alliance) to satisfy the short-term preferences of a majority of electors who are not located on the ideological fringe."<sup>[\[157\]](#cite_note-159)</sup> "The traditional *brokerage* model of Canadian politics leaves little room for ideology."<sup>[\[158\]](#cite_note-160)</sup>

[^cite_note-Canadian-295]: &#x20;All citizens of Canada are classified as "Canadians" as defined by [Canada's nationality laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_nationality_law). "Canadian" as an ethnic group has since 1996 been added to census questionnaires for possible ancestral origin or descent. "Canadian" was included as an example on the English questionnaire and "Canadien" as an example on the French questionnaire.<sup>[\[290\]](#cite_note-293)</sup> "The majority of respondents to this selection are from the eastern part of the country that was first settled. Respondents generally are visibly European (Anglophones and Francophones) and no longer self-identify with their ethnic ancestral origins. This response is attributed to a multitude or generational distance from ancestral lineage."<sup>[\[291\]](#cite_note-294)</sup>

[^cite_note-300]: &#x20;Indigenous peoples are not considered a visible minority in Statistics Canada calculations. Visible minorities are defined by Statistics Canada as "persons, other than Aboriginal peoples, who are non-Caucasian in race or non-white in colour".<sup>[\[295\]](#cite_note-299)</sup>
